EXPLANATION OF TECHNICAL TERMS. 39 


J’ Adoube-—A French phrase, meaning “I adjust,” used 
when touching a piece to place it right upon the board 
should it have been disturbed. It means that the player 
does not intend to move the piece he touches if it is one of 
his own, or to take it if it belongs to his opponent. See 
Law VI. 

Middle Game.—tThe portion of the game between the 
opening and the end game. 

Minor Piece.—The Bishops and Knights are called “ minor 
pieces,” to distinguish them from the Rooks and Queens, 
which are of greater value. 

Odds.—When a player gives an advantage to his oppo- 
nent at the commencement of the game, either by taking 
off any of his own forces or allowing him extra moves, &c 
he gives odds. 

Open File—A file upon which there is no piece or Pawn. 

Open Game—A term usually applied to all games in 
which, if the first player moves the Pawn to King’s fourth, 
his opponent answers with the same move. By doing so 
he subjects himself to an attack of more or less intensity 
and duration according to the opening chosen, whereas by 
adopting the “close game” the advantage of the first move 
is sooner neutralized. 

Opening.—The beginning of a game. Applied specially 
to different styles of commencing, as the “ King’s Knight’s 
Opening,” the “King’s Bishop’s Opening,” the “ Irregular 
Openings,” &c. 

Opposition —A peculiar position of the Kings opposed to 
each other, by which the winning, drawing, or losing of the 
game is in many cases decided. Fully explained subse- 
quently in the chapter on “ End Games.” 

Partie —A game. 

Passed Pawn.—A Pawn which has none of the adversary’s 


40 THE BOOK OF CHESS, 


Pawns in front or on either file next to the one on which it 
moves, so that it cannot be taken except by a piece. 
Perpetual Check.—When one of the players has no means 
of avoiding the repeated checks of his opponent the game 
is drawn by “ perpetual check.” White’s King is on King’s 
Bishop’s fifth, and his Queen on King’s eighth; Black’s 
King is on King’s Rook’s eighth, and he has Queens on 
King’s Rook’s seventh and King’s Knight’s eighth. Were 
‘it Black’s turn to move he would play Queen from King’s 
Rook’s seventh to King’s Bishop’s seventh, giving check ; 
White would have to play King to King’s sixth, and then 
Black moves either Queen to King’s eighth and next ex- 
changes Queens, winning easily. If, however, White were 
to move first he could draw the game by perpetual check. 
He would play Queen to King’s fourth, check; Black 
cannot escape with the King, and is therefore obliged to 
interpose one of his Queens, upon which White again 
checks at King’s square or King’s Rook’s fourth. Black 
can do nothing but interpose, and White continues to check 
on King’s square, King’s fourth, or King’s Rook’s fourth, as 
the rank, diagonal, or file is left open for his attack; the 
perpetual check cannot be avoided, and the game is drawn. 
Pin.—When a Knight is on the same diagonal as a Rook 
or Queen of the same colour, and an adverse piece is 
brought to attack it so that if moved away it will leave the 
Rook or Queen en prise, the Knight is said to be pinned. 
Similarly, pieces may be pinned against the King, and with 
greater power, as they are then unable to move away on 
account of discovering check. 
Position.—The state of a game at any particular time. 
Problem.—A position in which mate is to be accomplished 
in a definite number of moves. 
Wap, Protect —You “protect” a piece or Pawn when you move 


EXPLANATION OF TECHNICAL TERMS, 41 


another piece or Pawn so that you may take the attacking 
piece if it makes the capture threatened. 

Queentng— When a Pawn reaches the eighth rank you 
are entitled to exchange it for any piece you desire; this 
is called “queening” a Pawn. 

Scholar's Mate—A short game, named—like the Fool’s 
Mate—to denote that the player who loses is quite a be- 
ginner. It is accomplished as follows :— 


WHITE. BLACK. 
1. Pawn to King’s fourth. 1. Pawn to King’s fourth. 
2. King’s Bishop to Queen’s 2. King’s Bishop to Queen’s 
Bishop’s fourth. Bishop’s fourth. 
3. Queen to King’s Rook’s 3. Pawn to Queen’s third. 


fifth. 
4. Queen takes King’s Bishop’s Pawn. Mate. 


Smothered Mate——Can only be effected by the Knight, 
which is the only piece that can attack over the heads of 
other pieces or of Pawns. The adverse King is so sur- 
rounded by his own forces that there is not a single square 
to which he can move, even to go into check—he is com- 
pletely blocked in. Place the White King on King’s 
fourth, Queen on Queen’s sixth, and Knight on Queen’s 
Rook’s sixth, and the Black King on Queen’s Rook’s square, 
Rook on Queen’s Bishop’s square, and Pawns on Queen’s 
Knight’s second and Queen’s Rook’s second. White plays 
Queen to Queen’s Knight's eighth, check ; the Black King 
cannot take it, as it is supported by the Knight—he must 
therefore play Rook takes Queen, and White then moves 
Knight to Queen’s Bishop’s seventh, giving “ smothered 
mate.” See Introductory Game V. 

Stalemate—A position in which one of the players can 
move none of his pieces or Pawns, and is not at the time 
in check, but cannot move the King without going into 


42 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


check. Put the White King on King’s Bishop’s second, 
Bishop on Queen’s fifth, and Knight on King’s Bishop's 
third, and the Black King on King’s Rook’s eighth, and 
Pawn on Queen's third. If it is Black’s turn to play he 
cannot move the Pawn on account of the Bishop. in front 
of it, neither can he move the King without going into 
check; therefore, as he cannot move at all, and is not at 
present in check, he is “stalemated,” and the game is 
drawn. 

Study.—A position in which the correct termination is 
stated as a problem to be solved, but the number of moves 
in which it can be done is not mentioned. Usually applied 
to difficult end games, the object being to obtain such a 
position that the winning or drawing of the game is appa- 
rent at a glance. 

Superior Pieces—The Queen and Rook, as distinguished 
from the minor pieces, . 

Variation.—A deviation from the line of play adopted 
in any position, showing what the result of other moves 
would probably have been. 


LAWS OF THE GAME. 43 


CHAPTER IIL 
LAWS OF THE GAME. 


THE following laws were adopted at a meeting of. the 
British Chess Association, held on July 17th, 1862, during 
the Congress at London. Since that time they have been 
recognised as binding by a majority of the clubs through- 
out the United Kingdom, and may therefore be considered 
as having superseded the older laws. 


I—THE CHESS-BOARD, 


The board must be so placed during play that each com- 
batant has a white square in his right-hand corner. If, 
during the progress of a game, either player discovers that 
the board has been improperly placed, he may insist on its 


being adjusted. 
II.—TaHE CHESSMEN, 


If, at any time in the course of a game, it is found that 
the men were not properly placed, or that one or more of 
them were omitted at the beginning, the game in question 
must be annulled. If at any time it is discovered that a 
man has been dropped off the board, and moves have been 
made during its absence, such moves shall be retracted, 
and the man restored. If the players cannot agree as to 
the square on which it should be replaced, the game must 
be annulled. 


44 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


IIL—RIGHT oF MOVE AND CHOICE OF COLOUR. 


The right of making the first move, and (if either player 
require it) of choosing the colour which shall be retained 
throughout the sitting, must be decided by lot. In any 
series of games between the same players at one sitting, 
each shall have the first move alternately in all the 
games, whether won or drawn. In an annulled game the 
player who had the first move in that game shall move 
first in the next. 


IV.—ComMeENncina Out or TURN. 


If a player make the first move in a game when it is 
not his turn to do so, the game must be annulled if the 
error has been noticed before both players have com- 
pleted the fourth move. After four moves on each side 
have been made the game must be played out as it 
stands, 


V.—P Lavina Two Moves In SUCCESSION. 


Tf, in the course of a game, a player move a man when 
it is not his turn to play, he must retract the said move, 
and, after his adversary has moved, must play the man 
wrongly moved, if it can be played legally. 


VI—Tovcn anp Move. 


A player must never touch any of the men except when 
it is his turn to play, or except when he touches a man for 
the purpose of adjusting it, in which latter case he must, 
before touching it, say “I adjust,” or words to that effect. 


LAWS OF THE GAME. 45 


A player who touches with his hand (except accidentally) 
one of his own men when it is his turn to play, must 
move it if it can be legally moved, unless before touching 
it he say “I adjust” as above; and a player who touches 
one of his adversary’s men, under the same conditions, 
must take it if he can legally do so. If in either case the 
move cannot be legally made, the offender must move his 
King, but in the event of the King having no legal move 
there shall be no penalty. Ifa player hold a man in his 
hand, undecided on which square to play it, his adversary 
may require him to replace it until he has decided on its 
destination; that man, however, must be moved. Ifa 
player, when it is his turn to play, touch with his hand 
(except accidentally or in castling) more than one of his 
own men, he must play any one of them legally moveable 
that his opponent selects. If, under the same circum- 
stances, he touch two or more of the adversary’s men, he 
must capture whichever of them his antagonist chooses, 
provided it can be legally taken. If it happen that none 
of the men so touched can be moved or captured, the 
offender must move his King; but if the King cannot be 
legally moved there shall be no penalty. 


VIIL—Fause Moves Aanp ILLEGAL Moves, 


If a player make a false move, that is, either by playing 
a man of his own to a square to which it cannot be legally 
moved, or by capturing an adverse man by a move which 
cannot be legally made, he must at the choice of his oppo- 
nent, and according to the case, either move his own man 
legally, capture the man legally, or move any other man 
legally moveable. If in the course of a game an illegality 
be discovered (not involving a King being in check), and 


46 THE BOOK OF CHESS, 


the move on which it was committed has been replied to, 
and not more than four moves on each side have been made 
subsequently, all these latter moves, including that on 
which the illegality was committed, must be retracted. If 
more than four moves on each side have been made the 
game must be played out as it stands, 


VIIL.—Cuxcx. 


A player must audibly say “Check!” when he makes a 
move which puts the hostile King in check. The mere 
announcement of check shall have no signification if check 
be not actually given. If check be given but not an- 
nounced, and the adversary make a move which obviates 
the check, the move must stand. If check be given and 
announced, and the adversary neglect to obviate it, he 
shall not have the option of capturing the checking piece 
or of covering, but must “move his King” out of check ; but 
if the King have no legal move there shall be no penalty. 
If in the course of a game it be discovered that a King has 
been left in check for one or more moves on either side, 
all the moves subsequent to that on which the check was 
given must be retracted. Should these not be remembered 
the game must be annulled. 


TX.—ENFORCING PENALTIES, 


A player is not bound to enforce a penalty. A penalty 
can only be enforced by a player before he has touched a 
man in reply. Should he touch a man in reply in conse- 
quence of a false or illegal move of his opponent, or a false 
cry of check, he shall not be compelled to move that man, 
and his right to enforce a penalty shall remain. When the 
King is moved as a penalty it cannot castle on that move. 


LAWS OF THE GAME. 47 


X.—CASTLING. 


In castling the player shall move King and Rook simul- 
taneously, or shall touch the King first. If he touch the 
Rook first he must not quit it before having touched the 
King, or his opponent may claim the move of the Rook asa 
complete move. When the odds of either Rook or both 
Rooks are given, the player giving the odds shall be allowed 
to move his King as in castling, and as though the Rooks 
were on the board. 


XI—Covuntine Firry Moves. 


A player may call upon his opponent to draw the game, 
or to mate him within fifty moves on each side, whenever 
his opponent persists in repeating a particular check or 
series of checks, or the same line of play, or whenever he 
has a King alone on the board, or, 


King and Queen 

King ane Bishop against an equal or superior force. 
King and Knight | 

King and two Bishops 


King and two Knights \ against King and Queen, 
King, Bishop,and Knight 

and in all analogous cases ; and whenever one player con- 
siders that his opponent can force the game, or that 
neither side can win it, he has the right of submitting 
the case to the umpire or bystanders, who shall decide 
whether it is one for the fifty move counting; should 
he not be mated within the fifty moves he may claim 
that the game shall proceed. 


48 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


XIL—PAwWN-TAKING IN PASSING. 


Should a player be left with no other move than to 
take a Pawn in passing, he shall be bound to play that 
move. 


XITL—QUEENING A PAWN. 


When a Pawn has reached the eighth square, the 
player has the opportunity of selecting a piece, whether 
such piece has been previously lost or not, whose name 
and powers it shall then assume, or of deciding that it 
shall remain a Pawn. 


XIV.—ABANDONING THE GAME. 


If a player abandon the game, discontinue his moves, 
voluntarily resign, wilfully upset the board, or refuse to 
abide by these laws, or to submit to the decision of the 
umpire, he must be considered to have lost the game. 


XV.—THE UMPIRE OR BYSTANDERS, 


The umpire shall have authority to decide any ques- 
tion whatever that may arise in the course of a game, 
but must never interfere except when appealed to. He 
must always apply the laws as herein expressed, and 
neither assume the power of modifying them, nor of 
deviating from them in particular cases, according to his 

"own judgment. When a question is submitted to the 
umpire or to bystanders by both players, their decision 
shall be final and binding upon both players. The term 
bystander shall comprise any impartial player of eminence 
who can be appealed to, absent or present. 

a. 


OBSERVATIONS ON THE PIECES, 49 


CHAPTER IV. 


OBSERVATIONS ON THE PIECES. 


THE following observations are intended, as a matter of 
course, to be applied merely in a general way. In a mul- 
titude of positions the player will have to modify them in 
some degree, as occasion may require; practice and study 
alone will enable him to decide when to adopt and when 
to discard the principles here laid down. 


THE KING. 


The King is, as a matter of course, invaluable, and should 
therefore be rarely moved out as an aggressive piece early 
_ in the game, as, with most of the pieces on the board, he 
is liable to constant attack, and may be hemmed in and 
checkmated if advanced too far into the game. Young 
players should never forget that if they expose their King 
to a series of checks the necessity of defending him may 
- oblige them to sacrifice pieces, or will at least so cramp 
their game and bring out that of the opponent as to give 
the latter a winning game. On this account it is generally 
advisable to castle as early as possible, for not only is the 
King then placed in a position of comparative safety, but 
the player is enabled to get his pieces out, and commence 
an attack upon'the enemy. For the same reason it is 
often good play to give a series of checks, especially if you 
can force the adverse King to move, and thereby deprive 
him of his power of castling; and this purpose, it must 
not be forgotten, can be achieved by capturing « piece 

E 


50 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


or Pawn, compelling the Rook to retake, after which the 
King could not castle on that side, and also by placing a 
piece where it commands a square over which the King 
would have to pass in the action of castling. Remember 
that it is better to cover a check with a piece that attacks 
the checking piece, as it frequently gains you a move, 
owing to the necessity of your opponent to guard or remove 
the piece ; it is also preferable to interpose a piece of less 
value than the attacking piece. On the King’s side of the 
board, generally, the King is less liable to attack, and more 
easily protected, than on the Queen’s side; it is conse- 
quently better to castle with King’s Rook; but in many 
cases, when a strong attack is being organized against you, 
by castling on the opposite side you escape, and, quitting 
defensive for offensive operations, retort successfully upon 
your adversary. If you have castled on the King’s side, 
be watchful as to moving out the King’s Knight’s Pawn, as 
if you play it to Knight’s third the adverse Queen’s Bishop 
may be played to King’s Rook’s sixth, occupying a very 
dangerous position, from which it can only be dislodged 
with difficulty, if at all. Having castled, however, and 
with the Rook supporting the King’s Bishop’s Pawn, it is 
mostly advisable to push on the latter at the first oppor- 
tunity; this not only tends to break up your opponent’s 
centre, but it makes an opening for your Rook, and occa- 
sionally decides the position in yourfavour. If the Queens 
have been exchanged, it is generally better to play King to 
King’s Bishop’s second than to castle, as he is there out of 
danger, and ready to take a prominent part in the struggle 
when required ; and it must be remembered that after most 
of the pieces are off the board the King is of the utmost 
importance as regards his aggressive power, and that most 
frequently upon his good or bad manceuvring will depend 


OBSERVATIONS ON THE PIECES. 51 


the result. When your adversary has a Pawn immediately 
in front of your King, you should hesitate about taking it, 
as it is a much better protection for you against his attack 
than one of your own men can be; and you must also be 
careful, if you have castled, not to expose yourself to a 
mate from his Rook or Queen played to your first rank, 
checking, and your King unable to escape, owing to none 
of the Pawns in front having been moved. Beware of a 
discovered check. 


THE QUEEN. 


If it can be avoided, the Queen should never be employed 
to defend a piece, as, being the most powerful and valuable 
' of the pieces, she can be easily attacked and driven away 
by a guarded inferior piece, and loss will probably ensue 
unless you have others of your men bearing upon the point 
assailed. She should not be brought out too early, for 
your opponent can then attack her with his pieces, causing 
you to lose moves in providing for her safety, and deve- 
loping his own game. Do not take her too far away from 
the support of the King, as it may happen that she will be 
unable to get back in time to protect him; for this reason 
you must be particularly careful in taking a piece or Pawn 
with the Queen, it being most likely left en prise to get her 
away from where the attack will be made. Be mindful, 
in addition, not to make an onslaught with her unsup- 
ported, unless a direct benefit—such, for instance, as 
making the adverse King move if he have not castled—can 
be gained. If the Queen’s Rook, on its own square, is en 
prise of your Queen, do not too hastily snatch it, as after- 
wards she is liable to be confined there, and won. The 
Queens should not be exchanged for the sake of exchanging, 
—in fact, none of the pieces should; the finest games exe. 


52 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


the most complicated, and they are generally also the most 
instructive. Never play the-Queen in front of the King 
when there is a chance of her being pinned by a Rook; 
and be watchful of a diagonal pin against the King by a 
Bishop, or a fork of King and Queen by a Knight or Pawn. 
Remember the Queen’s power to give check, and at the 
same time attack a piece in the other direction of her 
move, winning it, 


THE ROOK. 


At home, on their own squares, the Rooks are of very 
little use in the great majority of games; hence arises the 
imperative necessity of getting out the rest of your pieces, — 
so that the Rooks may be brought into the field. It is sur- 
prising how frequently a game is over before the losing 
player has moved at least his Queen’s Rook; this is espe- 
cially the case when the second player. is receiving the 
odds of a piece; his opponent obtains an attack, and pur- 
sues it with such determination, aided by the weak moves 
on the other side, that the game is decided almost in the 
outset, and the ostensible receiver of the odds, instead of 
being so in reality, has in all probability been giving 
them, through his pieces being confined at home, where 
they are worse than useless. In any event, therefore, 
strive to get out your pieces, and bring the Rooks into play 
as soon as possible. If there should be an open file, take 
possession of it at once by playing one of your Rooks on to 
it; and if your antagonist then play one of his Rooks also 
upon it, challenging an exchange, it is most frequently 
better to decline the challenge, and support with your other 
Rook. If he take, you retake, still retaining possession of 
the file; whereas if you took first, his Rook with which he 
retook would be posted upon it. Endeavour to double 


OBSERVATIONS ON THE PIECES. 53 


your Rooks, that is, try to get one in front of the other 
on the same file, supporting each other; their power is 
materially increased when thus acting in conjunction. 
And, if possible, let the foremost Rook be on your adver- 
sarys second rank, as there it confines his King to the 
first rank, and also attacks the unmoved Pawns, which he 
‘must lose time in defending. In this position you also 
threaten to place both Rooks on this adverse second rank, 
which is the strongest position they can occupy, and in 
the majority of cases is quite decisive. With the Rook 
you must be careful of a diagonal pin by an adverse Bishop 
or a fork by a Knight, resulting in the loss of the “ex- 
change ;” and if one of your Rooks is unsupported, beware 
of the opposite Queen giving check and at the same time 
diagonally attacking your Rook, which will then be lost. 
Be watchful of the power of the Rook to pin a piece against 


the King. 
THE BISHOP. 


The importance of the Bishops at the outset of a game 
is very great, as they can be so readily moved out to attack 
the opponent’s entrenchments. At the beginning, the 
King’s Bishop is considered to be of slightly more value 
than the Queen’s Bishop, as the former can at once attack 
the King’s Bishop’s Pawn, which, being defended solely by 
the King, is the weakest, and also the most easily assailable. 
The best position, therefore, for the King’s Bishop is at 
Queen’s Bishop’s fourth, and if attacked it should be moved 
back to Queen’s Knight’s third. The next most attacking 
position is at Queen’s third, but the Bishop should not be 
played there before the Queen’s Pawn is moved; from this 
point it attacks the King’s Rook’s Pawn, and often with 
dangerous effect after the adverse King has caatled on that 


54 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


side; for if he play Pawn to King’s Knight’s third, to pro- 
tect his position, your Queen’s Bishop can go to King’s 
Rook’s sixth, attacking the King’s Rook on King’s 
Bishop’s square, and confining the King terribly. When 
your King’s Bishop is at Queen’s Bishop's fourth, your 
antayonist may play Queen’s Bishop to King’s third, wish- 
ing to exhange, but do not do so unless you particularly 
desire to retain the move in your hands, as when he retakes 
with King’s Bishop’s Pawn it 1s often the reverse of dis- 
advantageous to have the latter doubled on the King’s 
file, where it supports the centre, and his King’s Rook after 
castling has a fine opening; you should retreat your Bishop 
to Queen’s Knight’s third, and if he take you recapture 
with Queen’s Rook’s Pawn, bringing it nearer the centre to 
. your advantage, and also opening the file for your Queen's 
Rook. The best place for the Queen’s Bishop at the begin- 
ning is either at King’s third, neutralizing the attack of 
the adverse King’s Bishop, or at King’s Knight's fifth, 
_ pinning the Knight on King’s Bishop’s third against the 
Queen. When on the latter square, ifthe Pawn is moved to 
King’s Rook’s third to compel you to take the Knight, it 
is not always advisable to do so, but the Bishop may be re- 
treated to King’s Rook’s fourth, keeping up the pin. . Should 
he then play Pawn to King’s Knight’s fourth, you move 
Bishop to King’s Knight’s third, still occupying an attack- 
ing post; and his position on the King’s side is much 
weakened by the premature advance of his Rook’s and 
Knight’s Pawns, rendering it unsafe for him to castle on 
that side, or dangerous if he have already castled there- 
Sometimes, too, instead of further retreating the Bishop 
from the attack of the Knight’s Pawn, either it or the 
King’s Knight (if on King’s Bishop’s third) may be advan- 
fageously sacrificed for the two Pawns, leaving the adverse 


OBSERVATIONS ON THE PIECES, 55 


King greatly exposed. When your Pawns on the Queen’s 
side have been advanced, the Queen’s Bishop played to 
Queen’s Knight’s second or Queen’s Rook’s third will 
occupy a commanding position. Remember that the 
Bishops are mostly of the greatest service when used in 
conjunction with the Queen ; all three being able to attack 
from a distance, renders them very dangerous ifffavourably 
placed. Occasionally it may happen that your Bishop 
attacks the adverse Queen’s Rook’s Pawn when it has not 
been moved, and when it is not guarded ; be careful as to 
taking it, for if the Queen’s Bishop’s Pawn and Queen’s 
Knight’s Pawn are also unmoved the latter may be pushed 
one square, and then your Bishop cannot escape, and may 
be attacked and won. At the end of a game, a Bishop 
obstructs the onward march of adverse Pawns better than 
either a Rook or a Knight; and remember that at the 
conclusion two Bishops are much more powerful than two 
Knights, but one Bishop is inferior to one Knight, as the 
latter has the power of attacking on squares of either 
colour. If you are left with Pawns and one Bishop only, 
keep the former on squares of a different colour from that 
which the latter is on, for then the action of the Bishop is 
not interrupted, and it can prevent the opponent’s King 
approaching the Pawns; if, however, you have an inferior 
game, and are playing to make a draw, you should keep 
them all on the same colour, so that the Bishop may pro- 
tect them. Recollect the power of the Bishop to pin a 
piece against. the King or against a piece of superior value. 


THE KNIGHT. 


All calculations in which the Knight is concerned are 
difficult, on account of his power of attacking without 
being Limself lable to capture, and of overleaping, othe 


56 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


pieces. His check, also, cannot be obviated by the inter- 
position of any piece, and he has the power of forking no 
less than seven pieces at once on certain squares. This 
renders him peculiarly dangerous, and the young player 
should jealously examine every contingency likely to arise 
from his move; for, involving, as it does, so many contin- 
gencies, one or more of them is liable to escape notice 
unless great care be taken. The best place for the King’s 
Knight at the commencement is King’s Bishop’s third, 
where he attacks the adverse King’s Pawn if it has been 
moved two squares, and also prevents the opponent’s Queen 
being moved to King’s Rook’s fifth, which is often fraught 
with danger to your game. It is bad play to move King’s 
Knight to King’s second, through fear of obstructing the 
progress of the King’s Bishop’s Pawn, as on that square it 
confines the King’s Bishop and the Queen, the action of 
which is of far greater importance than is that of the 
King’s Bishop’s Pawn. From King’s Bishop’s third the 
Knight can also be played to King’s fifth or King’s Knight's 
fifth, assailing the King’s Bishop’s Pawn in conjunction 
with your King’s Bishop, which is frequently productive 
of a splendid attack, whether your opponent should have 

‘castled or not. The Queen’s Knight is well placed on 

Queen’s Bishop’s third, where it protects your King’s 
- Pawn when pushed two squares, and whence it can be 
played to Queen’s fifth or Queen’s Knight's fifth, threaten- 
ing to capture the Queen’s Bishop’s Pawn if it is left, un- 
guarded, giving check and winning the Queen’s Rook. If 
your Pawn has been played to Queen’s Bishop’s third, the 
Queen’s Knight, after moving out the Queen’s Bishop, 
may be moved to Queen’s second, where it will guard the 
King’s Knight if it should be pinned against the Queen 
by the adverse Queen’s Bishop. Should your Queen’s 


OBSERVATIONS ON THE PIECES. 57 


Knight be on Queen’s Bishop’s third, and there is no 
opening on the Queen’s side, it is frequently good play to 
move it to King’s second, and thence to King’s Knight's 
third, where it will exercise a great influence upon your 
opponent’s game, threatening to be played to King’s 
Bishop’s fifth, a most awkward move to parry. The 
Knights should seldom be played to the Rook’s files, for 
at the side of the board their power is much diminished, 
and they may possibly be captured by the adverse Bishops, 
doubling the Knights’ Pawns on the Rooks’ files, the worst 
position they can be in. They (the Knights) should not 
be ventured too far into the adversary’s game, as their 
retreat may be cut off, and they will be lost. If a Knight 
is on one of the corner squares, and the adverse King is 
within four squares—except it be on a side line—it can 
win the Knight in three moves. If a Knight be trouble- 
some in checking, place your King on the same diagonal, 
with one square between them, and check cannot be again 
given by the Knight in less than three moves; and if the 
King and Knight be on different colours, the latter cannot 
check in less than two moves. Be watchful for every 
chance of forking the adversary’s King and one or more of 
his pieces, and do not forget that if you give check with a 
Knight, and your opponent cannot take it, he must move 
his King, and is therefore unable to castle afterwards. It 
is also important to bear in mind that a single Knight is 
stronger than a single Bishop, with Pawns on each side, 
at the end of a game. 


THE PAWN. 
Pawn play is the essence of Chess, for, between evenly 
matched players, it is generally the conduct of these appa- 
rently insignificant pieces which decides the game. The 


58 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


first point to attend to is the establishment of your Pawns 
in the centre of the board, where they act as a powerful 
constraint upon the freedom of your antagonist’s game, 
while, your own pieces being unconfined, you can organize 
an attack at leisure. For the attainment of this object, 
you must of course push forward your Pawns so as not to 
retard the movements of your pieces. It is usual to com- 
mence by playing King’s Pawn to King’s fourth, and then 
endeavour to get the Queen’s Pawn to Queen’s fourth, as 
these are good positions for them to occupy. If they 
should be attacked by adverse Pawns, it is frequently better 
not to exchange, but to push the attacked Pawns onward; 
but they should not be advanced too early, as it is difficult 
to support them when they are too far forward at the 
beginning of a game—they are generally strongest at their 
fourth squares; and more especially when your opponent 
has not castled, and when you are in doubt as to the side 
on which to commit yourself to an attack. Pawn to 
Queen’s Bishop’s third is generally a good move, as it 
supports the Queen’s Pawn at Queen’s fourth, or prepares 
for its being played there; playing Pawn to King’s Bishop’s 
third, however, for the purpose of similarly supporting the | 
King’s Pawn, is bad play, for it weakens the King’s position 
and exposes him to attack, besides preventing the King’s 
Knight from coming out into the field to advantage. The 
safety of the King’s Bishop’s Pawn must be carefully 
attended to, as it is your weakest point, and is most readily 
assailable. After castling, and when your King’s Rook is 
at King’s Bishop’s square, supporting the King’s Bishop’s 
Pawn, you should seize the first favourable opportunity of 
playing the latter to King’s Bishop’s fourth, obtaining a good 
opening ; but the King’s Knight’s Pawn, in front of your 
King, should rarely be moved, unless some particular 
=. 


OBSERVATIONS ON THE PIECES. 59 


object is to be obtained. The Queen’s Pawn should seldom 
be played to Queen’s third before the King’s Bishop is out, 
as it restricts the latter to one square, King’s second, where 
it 1s of service only for defence, not for attack. Pawn to 
King’s Rook’s third is a strong defensive move, preventing 
Queen’s Bishop being played to King’s Knight’s fifth, pin- 
ning your Knight on King’s Bishop’s third against the 
Queen. Pawns being of less value than the pieces it 1s 
more advisable to defend with them than with the latter, 
unless doing so breaks up your position. Doubled Pawns 
at the side of the board are the reverse of advantageous, 
whereas in most cases a Pawn doubled towards the centre 
becomes of greater value; the Queen’s or King’s Knight’s 
Pawn doubled upon the Queen’s or King’s Rook’s file is 
thereby rendered comparatively worthless; but the Queen’s 
Rook’s Pawn doubled on the Queen’s Knight’s file gives an 
opening for the Queen’s Rook, and the King’s Bishop’s Pawn 
doubled on the King’s file not only supports the centre, but 
enables the King’s Rook, after castling on. that side, to enter 
into the game with great effect. Take care of your Pawns, 
but do not fancy that the loss of one necessarily implies the 
loss of the game; on the contrary, by sacrificing a Pawn, 
or even two, or more, at the right moment, you may obtain 
such an attack as will repay you, and with ample interest. 
Pawns, when united, have great strength at all times, but 
when isolated they are mostly weak; and if you have a 
diagonal line of Pawns you should be particularly careful 
as to the safety of the top one. A passed Pawn, supported 
by another, is almost, if not quite, equal to a piece in 
value; and it should never be forgotten that two united 
Pawns at the end of a game cannot be assailed with any 
effect by the adverse King, for if he take the lest ons he 
cannot reach the other afterwards, and it will queen, "wo 


60 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


Pawns against one mostly win, but you must be cautious 
how you exchange one of them for your adversary’s, 98 
when you are left with a single Pawn his King may be 
able to obtain the opposition, and you can then only draw. 
It must not be overlooked that the Pawn can fork two 
pieces with greater effect than any other of your 
pieces can; and remember that if you are left, at the end 
of a game, with Pawns only against a minor piece, you 
may win, but that your opponent can never do so, unless, 
indeed, his piece happen to be a Knight, and even then 
only under most exceptional circumstances. 


GENERAL OBSERVATIONS. 


There is little to add to the above remarks, except with 
reference to your demeanour when playing. Whether you 
are winning or losing you should strive never to show by 
your manner—exultant on the one hand and depressed on 
the other—that you feel conscious of it; and when the 
game is over, if you have lost, do not give way to peevish- 
ness and ill-temper, ascribing your defeat to every cause 
but the right one (which, you may depend upon it, your 
opponent knows well enough); or, if you have won, do not 
glory in it and make a parade of your success: if you are 
guilty in either respect you will only draw upon yourself 
the merited contempt or indignation of your antagonist. 
Chess is often said to be a test of a man’s ability, but this 
is true in @ limited sense only; you may be more gifted 
with the peculiar quality of mind that enables you quickly 
to understand the game, you may have devoted more time 
to its study, you may be in better health or more at ease 


OBSERVATIONS ON THE PIECES. | 61 


in mind than your adversary: any of these causes may 
enable you to achieve a victory, but they do not entitle 
you to insult him by offensively proclaiming, in deed if 
not in word, that you have beaten him. I write strongly 
upon this point—I hope unnecessarily so, but instances 
have been known of those who have degraded Chess by 
their immeasurable self-sufficiency. Your conqueror may 
perhaps pardon you if you attempt, within moderate limits, 
to explain a defeat; but suppose you are the conqueror, 
and that he whom you have vanquished is seeking relief 
from a secret care or sorrow in the charms of the fascinat- 
ing game—to which fact alone possibly you owe your vic- 
tory,—what could be more distressing to his feelings than 
an impertinent assertion, none’ the less evident because 
unexpressed in language, of your superior powers? I feel 
sure the hint will be sufficient—never detract from the 
merits of your adversary’s success, or exaggerate those of 
your own. I do not wish you to repress the pardonable 
elation consequent upon victory, but to repress the expres- 
sion of it in any way; you will then not only derive 
pleasure yourself, but give it to others also. When you 
are playing never allow your hand to hover over the board 
while undecided what move to make, and especially avoid 
the habit of placing a piece upon a square and keeping 
your finger upon it, so that, if on examination you find the 
move is unsuitable, you may play the piece to another 
square. This is not only an offensive habit as far as look | 
is concerned, but while your arm is stretched over the 
board you prevent your opponent from seeing all the pieces, 
when—if; wearied by your dilatory conduct, he play with- 
out consideration—he is liable to overlook something, and 
you gain an advantage; besides, by putting thepiece down | 
and then examining the probable result, you practically 


62 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


take one move more than does your antagonist, and this, 
repeated several times, is sufficient to decide the game, 
Every consequence of a move should, as far as possible, be 
examined as the pieces stand, and for my part I would 
make it compulsory upon a player, if he touched any 
square with a piece, to move it to that square. “Among 
the rules given by Lopez, by Gianutio (1597), and more | 
in detail by Lolli (1763), appears the regulation that s 
piece must be played on to a square it has touched, and 
that a piece played out on a row of squares may be pushed 
to one more forward, but must not be withdrawn to one it 
has already passed.” Thus far says Von Der Laza, but 
he remarks further that “this provision, in itself perhaps 
good, appears in the present day too stringent.” I do net 
think so, for it appears to me that great stringency in — 
repressing a contravention of fair play is perfectly justified 
The objection that passing such a rule would imply a want 
of belief in the gentlemanly feeling of some Chess-players 
is groundless, for several of the existing rules do so in 
quite as great a degree, and he who is guilty of such con- 
duct deserves to suffer a penalty, while he who is not 
guilty of it could not feel hurt at the adoption of a law 
which cannot apply to him. It is already enacted that “if 
a player hold a man in his hand, undecided on which 
square to play it, his adversary may require him to replace 
it until he has decided on its destination.” This is very 
well so far as it goes, but it does not go far enough in lear 
ing the “requiring” optional with the adversary, and in no 
providing a penalty if the piece is played to a square 
without the hand being removed. When it is your turn 
play you should well study the position, and, having maée 
up your mind, act upon the decision at once and without 
hesitation. Learn to play indiscriminately with either the 


OBSERVATIONS ON THE PIECES. 63 


white or the black pieces, and never imagine you can use 
a particular piece better than you can others, or, seeking to 
preserve it, you may get into sad trouble. Do not object 
to accept odds from a superior player, so that the game 
may be made interesting to him as well as to yourself; 
and, above all, play always according to the strict rules. 
To sum up:—If you cannot evade an attack endeavour to 
obtain a perpetual check or to compel the withdrawal of 
some of the attacking pieces to prevent it; recollect that 
an attack may frequently be parried, or repulsed altogether, 
by forcing an exchange of pieces; bring all your pieces 
into play without loss of time, and do not put them in 
each other’s way; remember defence as well as attack, 
that is, in defending endeavour to do so in such manner 
that you in turn attack, and in attacking do it so that if 
repelled you may be able effectually to defend your own 
position ; and, lastly, never give check without a definite 
object in view. 


THE OPENINGS. 


The following are the great divisions under which the 
various modes of opening the game are classified :— 

1. Kine’s KnyicuT’s OpENING.—Both players for their 
first move play King’s Pawn to King’s fourth, and the first 
player then brings out his King’s Knight to King’s Bishop’s 
third. 

2. Kine’s BisHor’s OpENING.—After King’s Pawn to 
King’s fourth on each side, the first player moves 2. King’s 
Bishop to Queen’s Bishop’s fourth. 

3. QUEEN'S BisHor’s Pawn’s OPENING.—After 1. King’s 
Pawn to King’s fourth each, the first player moves 2.Queen’s 
Bishop’s Pawn to Queen’s Bishop’s third. 

4. CENTRE Gamsit.—After 1. King’s Pawn to Kings 


64 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


fourth each, the first player moves 2. Queen’s Pawn to: 
Queen’s fourth. 

5. Hampr’s OpEninG.—After 1. King’s Pawn to King’s 
fourth each, the first player moves 2. Queen’s Knight to 
Queen’s Bishop’s third. 

6. Kine’s Gamsrr.—After 1. King’s Pawn to King’s 
fourth each, the first player moves 2. King’s Bishop’s Pawn 
to King’s Bishop’s fourth. 

7. CENTRE CounTER Gampit.—After the first player 
moves 1. King’s Pawn to King’s fourth, his opponent 
answers with 1. Queen’s Pawn to Queen’s fourth. 

8. FRENCH OPENING.—After 1. King’s Pawn to King’s 
fourth on the part of the first player, his opponent answers 
with 1. King’s Pawn to King’s third. 

9. SIcILIAN OPpENING.—After 1. King’s Pawn to King’s 
fourth by the first player, his opponent moves 1. Queen’s 
Bishop’s Pawn to Queen’s Bishop’s fourth. 

10. QUEEN’s GamBIT.—When each player moves 1.. 
Queen’s Pawn to Queen’s fourth, and the first player then 
moves 2. Queen’s Bishop’s Pawn to Queen’s Bishop's 
fourth. 

11. IRREGULAR OPENINGS.—Any initiatory moves, on 
either side, not mentioned above. 

12. There are besides the PAWN AND Mover, Pawn AnD 
Two MovEs, and PAWN AND THREE MovEs OPENINGS, when ~ 
the several odds named are given. 

As a matter of course there are various branches of the 
above openings, which have each a distinctive title, as 
will be ‘seen on reference to the Synopses introducing - 
Chapters VI., VII, VIII, and IX. These titles, I may 
here observe, should be thoroughly learnt, as constant refer- 
ence is made to them.

CHAPTER V. 
INTRODUCTORY GAMES. 


THE following games are intended to accustom the student 
to playing from the “book,” the notation adopted at first 
being more expanded than that in general use. They will 
also practically teach the various technical phrases which 
occur, and give an idea as to how the game is played. 


GaME I. 
Wurrs (AvutTHOR). Brack (AMATEUR). 


1. Pawn to King’s fourth 1. Pawn to King’s fourth 

Each player moves out his King’s Pawn first, in order to liberate 
the Queen and the King’s Bishop. 

2. Pawn to King’s Bishop’s 

fourth 

White plays out this Pawn, offering to sacrifice it, because if it

can deploy his pieces, which amply compensates for the loss.

[The position is peculiar and interesting. If White play R takes 
R, Black mates in four moves; if P takes R, it is mate. in three 
moves; and if Kt to K 4th or K B 3rd, Black takes Kt with K BR, 
and forces mate in at most six moves, excepting in the variation 
when White plays 24. B to K B 4th, which, however, delays the 
result but a short time longer. The student should endeavour to 
work out all these variations, as they are remarkably inetructive. ]

Black plays the Bishop here with the intention of preventing 
White from castling on the King’s side, at least for some time; it 
is, however, a bad move, as the Bishop can be immediately driven 
away, and the Gambit Pawn must fall. 

4. P to Q 4th 

Cutting off the action of the Bishop, and getting White’s Pawns 
well placed in the centre. 

4. B to Q Kt 5th (ch) 

Worse than useless; Black can inflict no damage, and the only 
result of this move is that he brings out the Queen’s Bishop’s 
Pawn, supporting the Queen’s Pawn, and also opening the diagonal 
for White’s Queen. 

5. P to Q B 3rd 5. B to Q R 4th 

Were Black to play B to Q 3rd, intending to support the ad- 
vanced King’s Bishop’s Pawn, White would answer with P to K 5th, 
and then take Pawn with Bishop.

Better than P to K 5, attacking the King’s Knight, which can- 
not be removed without leaving the Queen en prise; Black, how- 
ever, would move P to K BR 3rd, in turn attacking a piece; and 
if the Bishop retreated to K R4th, P to K Kt 4th would send it to 
K Kt 3rd, and Black could then remove the Knight from danger. 

8. Kt to Q B 3rd 

To get out his pieces; but P to Q B 3rd, threatening P to Q 4th, 
would have been a much better move. 

9. Castles 9. Bto Q Kt 3rd | 

The object of this move is to pin the Queen’s Pawn against the 
King, rendering it useless for the support of a piece or Pawn 

layed to K 5th; for instance, were ite now to move Kt to 
EK 2th, Black takes it with his Queen’s Knight for nothing, as 
Pawn cannot retake without discovering check.

19. B takes Q 
20. Kt to K B 7th. Mate. 


[This beautiful position is a familiar instance of “smothered 


INTRODUCTORY GAMES. 81 


mate,” the adverse King being prevented by his own pieces from 
escaping. It is the same as the celebrated “ Philidor’s Legacy,” 
and is frequently obtained inst amateurs of moderate skill. 
It should be noticed that if White on the fifteenth move did not 
take Queen’s Pawn when discovering check, Black, after taking 
the King’s Bishop with Knight, would play P to Q 4th, and the 
attack would be in a great measure paralyzed. And on the seven- 
teenth move White could not sacrifice the Queen at once, for, the 
Knight not commanding K Kt 8th, Black would make the capture 
with his King, arid escape. The beauty of the position consists 
of White’s ability by the series of checks to bring his Knight to 
K B 6th, compelling Black to “ smother” himself.]

[In this game White gives the odds of Queen’s Rook, which 
must be removed from the board before commencing to play. It 
is a fine example of the danger of bringing out the Queen too 
early; by means of continually attacking her, White is enabled 
speedily to get all his pieces into full play, while those of his oppo- 
nent remain at home in inglorious inactivity. It will be observed 
that Black, out of twenty moves, is compelled to play his Queen no 
less than eleven times. |

PHILIDOR’S DEFENCE. 


This defence consists in Black playing, on his second move, P to 
Q 3rd, supporting his King’s Pawn, which is attacked by the White 
Knight. It was first introduced by Ruy Lopez, a priest of Zafra, 
in Estremadura, Spain, who, in a work upon the game published 
in 1561, advocated this as the best defence in the King’s Knight's 
Opening. And Philidor, in 1749, expressed himself so strong yin 
favour of this move that, fora length of time, it was believed that the 
first player, by moving 2. Kt to K B 3rd, must have at the least an 
inferior game. The former, Ruy Lopez, believed that by defending 
with 2. Es to QB 3rd Blackexposed himself to a strong attack by the 
answer, on the part of White, 3. B to Q Kt 5th—which has been 
since known as “The Knight’s Game of Ruy Lopez; ” while Phili- 
dor imagined that by adopting 2. P to Q 3rd, the second player 
will infallibly be enabled to establish his Pawns in the centre. 
After the death of the celebrated French master, however, this 
opinion began to be controverted, and the contest between the 
opposite schools was maintained with great pertinacity, until, in 
1843, Von Bilguer and Heydebrand von der Laza, in their “ Hand- 
buch,” said, “ Black’s centre Pawns can always be broken up to the 
advantage of his adversary, and it follows, therefore, that P to Q 
drd is not a good defence, since it shuts in the Bishop at K B aq, 


PHILIDOR’S DEFENCE. 91 


while White, by 3. B to Q B 4th, or, still better, 3. P to Q 4th, can 
freely develop his game.” This is undoubtedly true, but it has 
never been proved that the Philidor Defence is unsafe; on the 
contrary, since the introduction of the brilliant attack of the 
“Evans Gambit” the best players are becoming more partial to the 
move, and its resources have become more fully understood. Mr. 
Boden well expresses the merits of the case when he says, “ Our 
conclusion respecting the Philidorian Defence is that it cannot be 
considered untenable. It cannot be denied that it gives the second 

layer a close game—that you will get an attacking situation. But 
be it remembered that a cramped game is not necessarily a lost one 
—no such thing. He must be content to conduct a close defence, 
and develop gradually.” The début, I may mention, is considered 
the closest of all the open games and the most open of all close 


games.

if 3. Kt to Q B 3rd, you move 4. P to Q B 3rd; if 3. Kt to K B 3rd, 
you move 4. Kt to K Kt 5th; and if 3. B to K 2nd, you move 
4. Kt to QB 3rd—in each case having a somewhat better position. 
For Black’s move 3. P to K B 4th, see Var. a.

12. Kt to Q 4th 


Having his pieces well deployed, White now commences an attack; by this 
move he threatens to take the Queen’s Bishop with the Knight and then to 
capture the Q B P with the Queen, and also threatens to play 18. Kt to K 6tb, 
attacking both Queen and Rook, and winning the “exchange.” 


12. Q to Q 2nd 
Guarding against both dangers. 
13. QB to Q sq 13. RB to K B 2nd 


Still apprehensive of Kt to K 6th, and desirous perhaps of playing QB to 
K B sq, ae ubling the Rooks, although it would not improve his game. This was 
a lost move, for the Rook is soon compelled to retreat. 
14. P to K B 4th 14. P to Q RB 4th 
Another lost move, giving White time to mature his attack. 


15. P to K B dth 15. K Rto K B sq 
To enable him to bring his King’s Knight into play, which he could not do 
while the Rook was on K B 2nd without leaving the latter piece en prise, 
16. Kt to K 6th 
The Knight here exercises a great influence in preventing the development of 


Black’s game. 
16. R to K Kt sq 
17. PtoQ BR 4th 
Highly necessary, to prevent P to Q Kt 4th, a move which would relieve Black’s 
game exceedingly.

. minoves. This position well shows the value of “time” in Chess: could Black 
gain one move, he might recover, but, being unable to do so, he must lose.

PETROFFS DEFENCE. 


This is called after the celebrated Russian player, M. Petroff, who 
urged its adoption as the best reply to White’s move of the King’s 
Knight. It is now, however, deemed inferior to 2. Kt to Q B 3rd, 
although it may be played without any great danger.

QUEEN BisHor’s PAWN GAME IN THE Kina KNIGuHT’s 
OPENING. 


From a consideration of the foregoing defences it will be seen 
that in all of them White obtains an attack of more or less intensity, 
and that it is with difficulty Black can obtain an equal game, and, 
indeed, sometimes impossible. We have, then, the variations 
when, abandoning the preceding unsatisfactory modes of play, 
he adopts the reply recommended as best—2. Kt to Q B 3rd. 
White, after this, has a number of ways in which he can carry on 
his game, and first of all we will examine 3. P to Q B 3rd, which, 

I

openings; it is considered, next to the Ru » the most 
dangerous mode of attack in its nature in the Hing ight’s Open- 
ing, and, notwithstanding the great amount of attention lately 
given to it, its resources are yet by no means thoroughly known.

Ruy Lopez Kyicut’s GAME. 

This is perhaps the most powerful attack which can be obtained 
by the first player without previously making a sacrifice, and for 
this reason it is the safest that can be adopted, for, if not suc- 
cessfal, you come out of the struggle with at the least equal 
position and forces, whereas in the gambits, if your attack tail, 


122 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


you are left with an inferior game, and Black ought to win. It 
was fear of this mode of play on the part of the first player that 
led Ruy Lopez, the Spantard, to reject the defence for Black of 
2. Kt to Q B 3rd, and to recommend 2. P to Q 3rd. And even 
up to the present day the correct play for Black is a point of con- 
troversy. The steady concentration of the attack, as op to 
the violent and fierce assaults of the King’s and Evans bits, 
is so deceptive that often the e is virtually over before tho 
second player has any idea that he is in danger, and consequently 
the test care is requisite on his part, not only in the opening 
itself, but also when the contest is faradvanced. Unless a mistake 
be made by White, the utmost his opponent can hope for is an 
even e, and one weak move alone may almost hopelessly com- 
promise his position, as is well shown in the Illustrative Game VIIL, 

tween Messrs. Lowenthal and Morphy. It is to be remembered, 
however, that White should be content to await the sure develo 
ment of his forces, and the advantage in position which he is lik 
to obtain; if he seek to make a vehement onset he will probably 
expose himself to a counter attack, from which escape without 
loss will be difficult. See Introductory Game III., between an 
Amateur and the Author.

White, it will be seen, threatens to capture the Knight and then 
take the King’s Pawn. Black has various modes of play now 
open to him, and we will first examine those in which, directly or 
indirectly, he at once protects the Pawn, and afterwards consider 
those in which he seeks to develop his game. See Variations 
A, B, C, and D.

ScoTcH GAMBIT. 


This variety of attack, which consists in the first player, on 
move 3, offering his Queen’s Pawn for capture, was compara 
tively unthought of until 1824, when the London Club adopted it 
in @ correspondence game against Edinburgh. The latter won, 
and reto with the same opening, and success awaited them in 
the attack as it had previously done in the defence. Hence the 
name of the gambit. It is undoubtedly a strong opening, for 
Black is compelled to accept the gambit, or he gets a bad position, 
and the legitimate result appears to be, with the best play on both 
sides, a drawn game.

Two KNIGHTS’ DEFENCE. 


Having examined those openings in which White does not play 
3B to Q B 4th, we must now look into the consequences of 
the adoption of this move. It is quite a safe mode of attack, and 
used perhaps more frequently than any other. Black has in 
Teply a choice of several moves, and first we will consider his 
answer of 3. Kt to Q B 3rd, known as the “ Two Knights’ Defence.” 
The characteristic of this opening is that Black brings out his 
two Knights on his second and third moves. It is a perfectly 
Sound defence, for if White seek to gain an advantage in the open- 
ing from an immediate attack he can only win a Pawn, and must 

submit to such an inferiority of position that with 
dificulty will he be enabled to make a draw.

HUNGARIAN GAMBIT, 


This defence was first mentioned by Cozio, but it was not known 

have been adopted in actual play until a celebrated match 
tween Pesth and Paris, when the Hungarians played it, with 
e intention, no doubt, of throwing their opponents entirely upon 
eir own resources. It is considered to be a safe move, although 
amping to the second player’s game.

Grvoco PIANO. 


In this opening Black replies to your move of 3. B toQ B 4th 
with the same, leading to most instructive positions, the result, 
with good play on each side, being a drawn game. No 
attack is obtained, but the pieces on each side are gradually de- 
veloped; for this reason the opening has always been a favourite 
one, and many fine games showing its various features have been 
preserved.

Evans GAMBIT. 


This magnificent variation of the attack in the Giuoco Piano 
bears the name of ite inventor, Captain Evans, R.N. From 
days of M‘Donnell and De la Bcurdonnais it has been an especial 


, it 
seems to be admitted that against the best moves on the part of 
the second player it must fail, yet the resources of the attack are 
so immense, and the defence so rtionately difficult and irk- 
some, that over the board it ma be pon wi 
test. probability of success. ite, by the sacrifice of hs 
¢ Kt P on the fourth move, is able immediately to establish his 
awns in the centre, and has such a free opening for his Ques, 
Queen’s Bishop, and Queen’s Knight, that their combined attack 
in most cases not to be parried, and the situations resulting are of 
& singularly intricate and beautiful nature.

24. Rto K Kt sq 
If White exchange Rooks and Queens, his K R must be won for Black’s Bishop 
or Knight. By moving the Rook he tempts Black to take it at once with the 
Bishop, in which case White would retake with Kt, and Black’s Knight could not 
escape.

TWO BISHOPS’ OPENING. 181 


CHAPTER VIL. 
THE KING’S BISHOP’S OPENING. 


WILIDOR considered this the best and safest mode of opening 
e game, but it must be remembered that his analysis—since 
oved to be faulty—appeared to show that if the first player 
oved 2. Kt to K B 3rd he would havea bad position. The weight 
opinion in the present day is in favour of the King’s Knight’s 
sening as being more sound and enduring in its attack. But 
e King’s Bishop’s Opening is thoroughly safe, and most interest- 
g and critical positions arise from it. 


Two BISHOPS’ OPENING. 


WHITE. Buack. 
1. Pto K 4th 1. Pto K 4th 
2. Bto Q B 4th 


The object of bringing out the Bishop is to enable you to occupy 
3 centre with your Pawns, which Philidor was of opinion you 
uld be able to do through the Bishop not offering any obstruc- 
n to their progress, as, he contended, the King’s ight did 
en played to K B 3rd before moving the K B P. Black has 
uw replies to your move—2. B to Q B 4th, 2. Kt to K B 3rd, 
Pto K B 4th, and 2. P to QB 8rd. The first, which some 
ieve to be the best, we will first examine, and consider the rest 
»sequently. 


2. B to Q B 4th 
3. P to QB 8rd (Var. D) 

White at once prepares to push on his Queen’s Pawn. Black 
} several moves he may lay in answer—3. Kt to K B 8rd, 
Q to K 2nd (see Var. AN, . Q to K Kt 4th (see Var. B), 
Kt to Q B 3rd, and 3. P to Q 4th (see Var. C). If he play 
Kt to Q B 8rd you move 4. P to Q 4th, upon which he retreats 
o.Q Kt 3rd, and the game is even. 


8. Kt to K B 8rd 
4. P to Q 4th 4. P takes P 
f he retreat B to Q Kt ord you take his K P,and on his playing, 
takes P you move Q to Q Sth, with much the better game.

very bad position.

In this position, however, White is still the exchange short, and his Queen is 
out of play, while Black has a safe game. The move selected, therefore, keeping 
the attack in his own hands, is much better,as Black’s Knight may quite asW 
be off the board as where it is. The student should learn from this never (0 
waste time in seeking to capture useless pieces, even if numerically inferior.

MISCELLANEOUS OPENINGS. 


The openings previously analyzed arose from White playing out 
his King’s Ku ft or Bishop on his second move; he may aio play 
2. P to K B 4th, forming the King’s Gambit. But before taking 
this into consideration we must examine three other modes of play 
which may be adopted, 2. P to Q B 3rd, 2. P to Q 4th, and 2. Kt to 
Q B 3rd. These are by no means seldom played, although not 
thought to be so attacking as many of the preceding variations.

CENTRE GAMBIT, 


Until of late years this opening was seldom adopted and but 
little known, but it is now in much favour, and is an instructive 
method of commencing a game. As a matter of course the posi- 
tions resulting from it have a great tendency to merge into ones 
analogous to those in the Scotch Gambit, which may be advan- 

—wiigoously studied in. connection with this opening,

DANISH GAMBIT. 


Von H. der Laza, the Prussian Ambassador at Copenhagen, 
writing to the Leipsic Schachzeitung in 1867, remarks of this 
gambit :—“I consider it deserves more attention from Chess- 

layers than it has hitherto received. The opening seems over- 

old for White at first sight, but a closer examination will show 
that in many variations, which at the first glance appear to be 
quite safe, the second player would soon involve his game in posi- 
tions quite untenable. . . . The Copenhagen players have 
hitherto pointed out only a few variations; they have not yet 
established an exhaustive analysis. The opinion, however, pre 
vails amongst them that the defence must preponderate. Some 
of them object to go so far in the attack as to take Knight’s Pawn 
with Pawn; on the contrary, they think it better to be satisfied 
with the Pawn gained, and now develop their game by playing 
either P to Q B 7th or Kt to QB 8rd” 


207 


DANISH GAMBIT.

CHAPTER VIII. 
THE KING'S GAMBIT. 


eEYOND doubt, the multifarious variations arising from this 
ening are the finest and most beautiful that can arise in Chess, 
d for this reason the King’s Gambit has ever been an especial © 
vourite with the best players of all times. The object of the 
crifice of the Pawn on the second move is to enable the first 
ayer rapidly to occupy the centre of the board with his Pawns, 
a at once to take up an attacking position. By the nature of 
e game the sacrifice should lose, but in actual play, so intricate 
d difficult are the positions evolved that without the most ex- 
me care on the part of the defence the first player will obtain 
2 advantage.

th be examined subsequently.

7. PtoQB 8rd 
The moves up to this point, both for attack and defence, are 
considered by the authorities the best that can be adopted. Black 
has now no less than nine different modes of defence, but the 
several variations are much too long for insertion in this work, 
and I must, therefore, confine myself to that which is laid down as 
the most advisable method.

Mozio GAMBIT. 


This magnificent opening is evidently derived from the Salvi 
Gambit, for White, seeing the counter attack to which he is sub- 
jected by moving his Knight w K Sth on ita being attacked, 
_, 


MUZIO GAMBIT. 237 


determines to give the piece up, and, bringing his other forees at 
once into play, endeavour to crush his opponent before he can 
develop his game. And the sacrifice appears to be quite sound, 
for, jadging from the analysis, the utmost Black can hope for, if 


the attack be conducted properly, is a drawn game; at the same 
time, one lost move on his part is sufficient to decide the contest.

pat opening, and the result of their labours has been to show 
1at in all proba ili it is a perfectly safe attack. If Black defend 

by checking with his Queen on the third move he is subjected 
»& most g assault for a length of time, and can barely hope 
» make a drawn game in the end; while if he give up the bit 
awn he cannot secure any advantage sufficient to decide the 

»ntest.

This is the “ Kieseritzky Defence” to the King’s Bishop’s Gambit. The object 
of the sacrifice of the Pawn is to prevent the attack of the Bishop upon the 
K B P, and eventually, by advancing P to Q B 8rd and P to Q 4th, to break up 
White’s position in the centre.

In “Hore Divaniane,” published in 1852 y Mr. Elijah Williams, White's 
move at this point is given as K to Kt 2nd. Had this, wever, been play 
Black, by moving 20. B to Q Kt 2nd, would have averted the t mate, 
and in all probability won the e. Captain Kennedy informs me, “ I knew 
the game before it was printed by Williams, or, indeed, Printed at all, and di- 
tinct 7 recollect that White’s move in question was, as I have stated, 20. Kt 
K 3nd.”

And Black has won two Pawns. Mr. Staunton goes on to observe that “it 
denotes great carelessness or some want of perception in both players that the

@ simple mistake in taking down the game, the eighteenth and nineteenth moves

18. B to Q B 2nd 
19. Bto K R 5th 19. R to K 3rd 
We have now precisely the eame position as in the above variation; ad 
through the simple alteration of the order of the moves we perceive that th 
mode of play suggested was never possible, which is, I think, much more co- 
ceivable than that two players like Morphy and Lowenthal both missed seeing 
such a palpable and easy combination. Black’s last move, nevertheless, was 10 
a good one; Q to K RB 5th would have been much better.

CENTRE COUNTER GAMBIT. 285 


CHAPTER IX. 


ve hitherto examined only those openings in which White 
by moving P to K 4th, and Black replies with the same 
yn his pat. We now come to those in which a variation is 
m his first move by either of the players. In the previous 
#3 it will have been noticed that Black is from the com- 
nent subject to an attack of more or less intensity; but this 
the case in the majority of the succeeding openings, for, by 
g to play 1. P to K 4th, Black prevents an attack being 
lately organized, and reduces the game to something like its 
; state, when the Pawns .were restricted to one square on 
rst move, and the encounter was decided, not by a dashing, 
ous assault, but by a series of crafty manceuvres and am- 
es. This possibly gives a higher tone to the partie, but it 
rives it of much of the interest inseparably associated with 
old contest.

SICILIAN OPENING. 


This opening was tly favoured by Philidor, and since b 

ala 5 opens Staunton, Jaenisch, ch, Anderssen, and other fins 
syers, and was for long thought perfectly safe. In 1851, how- 
er, new variations were introduced, which showed that the first 
ayer obtains a slight advantage, : and the French Opening is now 
nsidered better. But the Sicilian still possesses many attrac- 

ms for proficients, and the positions arising are most interesting.

Pawn with Pawn, and, on White playing 3. Kt to K B 3rd, we

an equal game. It is important to notice also that as a genera 
rule it is not good to advance either of the Knights’ Pawns one 
square when the King’s Pawn has been played two squares on the 
t move.

GAME LI. 


Between Messrs. Morphy and Carr, the former conducting eight 
games at once, without seeing any of the boards or men.

Black, no doubt, made this bizarre defence for the purpose of confusing his 
blindfold opponent; the result, however, was the reverse of what was intended, 
for his own game was brought into such a state of confusion in a very short time 
that he had no possibility of saving it. A blindfold player has really almost as 

pertoct a sight of the board in his mind es his antagonist playing with it before 
im; hence the uselessness of speculating on unusual moxyes tAding the latter.

THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


QUEEN’S GAMBIT ACCEPTED. 


The Queen’s Gambit is undoubtedly an excellent opening, lead- 
ing to positions of the greatest interest; but for some reason or 
other the leading players of the present day, with perhaps the 
single exception of Herr Harrwitz, appear to be by no means fond 
of it. There is certainly nothing like the variety in it which 
results from the openings on the King’s side, but it 1s by no means 
tame, and might well take the place in estimation at present held 
by the close irregular openings.

QUEEN’S GAMBIT DECLINED. 


It is agreed by the best players that it is more advisable to &- 
cline the gambit than to accept it, but this is perhaps more 
a matter of inclination than anything else. In either case the 
result appears to be an even game.

differently; and Mr. Léwenthal observes, “ Mr. Morphy agrees with me in the 
opinion that at this stage of the game the text move is the most forcible one that 
can be selected; indeed, he says he found it so strong that in his rhe a 
games with Mr. Harrwitz he preferred meeting 1. P to Q 4th with 1. P t 


B 4th, in place of exposing himself to this attack.” These diametrically oppo- 
site opinions are puzzling; but ‘‘ when doctors differ who shall decide ?”

GAMES AT ODDS. 315 


CHAPTER X. 


GAMES AT ODDS. 


EN players of different degrees of skill, at an early age in 
story of the game, various modes of reconciling the disparity 
introduced, consisting, naturally, in the stronger player 
‘off one or other of his pieces or Pawns, and the varieties of 
riven descend in minute gradations, suited to all degrees of 
th .To receive the odds of a Queen or of Rook and Knight, 
ven @ first-rate player, implies little skill on the of him 
>celves it, but strong amateurs will find a great difficulty in 
g over the odds of a Rook or a Knight when given by a 
xd practitioner. Without an express arrangement to the 
iry, the player giving these odds is entitled to the first move 
sry game, and, commencing an attack from the very begin- 
he takes the utmost advantage of each weak move made by 
ponent, until at length, through the superior development 
| forces, he crushes him as it were, before the extra piece 
to tell. When receiving the above odds, the player will of 
' conduct his attack or defence, according as he has first 
or not, the same as in the ordinary openings. And here I 
enter a protest against the advice usually given to a young 
' to exchange at every opportunity when in the receipt of 
odds, as then his extra piece becomes, when the board is 
d, of comparatively greater value. This is most pernicious 
, In my opinion, for any one adopting such a practice affords 
lf no opportunity of studying and profiting by the fine com- 
ons of his experienced antagonist; and though, when 
ing the odds, he may win the game (by pure brute force, so 
), he will never be able to rise above a dull level of medio- 
On the other hand, he who endeavours to beat skill by 
ar skill (the piece given making up for the inequality in this 
t) will rapidly improve, and in time may encounter the same 
lary, and successfully, upon even terms. I here speak, it 
be remembered, against exchanging for the mere sake of 
left at the end of the e with a greater force; I say 
ig against exchanging when it is necessary to repel a dan- 
3 attack—this is a legitimate exchange, and your opponent 
calculate before he commences his attack in what manner he 
event you from making it; but it is annoying to see a man 
re his antagonist of all chance of combining is pieces, wd 
boast of having achieved a victory. “Victory wdiem\ LOK 


316 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


much chance, although he beats the good player, would he have 
against one who loses to the latter in a skilfully played game? He 
would be crushed if playing on even terms with one who is, 
judging by the respective scores against the fine player, much 
weaker than himself. When an amateur is able to make even 
mes, receiving the odds of a Knight from such proficients as 
‘Donnell, De la Bourdonnais, Morphy, Staunton, Anderssen, 
Lowenthal, and others of like calibre, he is considered very strong; 
and if he have got over these odds, and make a good fight at the 
Pawn and Two Moves or the Pawn and Move, he is then a very 
fine player indeed. But in the great majority of cases such a 
de of proficiency as this is only attained after years of practice 
and close study; and it is very doubtful, if not even more than 
doubtful, whether any one, without a peculiar aptitude for the 
me, could ever rival such first-rate players as those mentioned. 
en promoted from the Knight to the Pawn and Two Moves, 
the amateur must expect for a length of time to be decisively 
beaten, the advantage given him beg not so much in material 
force as in position, which at first he will have a difficulty in success- 
fully utilising; but he must not be dismayed—every game he 
plays will tend to his improvement, and at length he will be able 
at these odds to cope upon something like equal terms with his 
formidable antagonist. this chapter will be found the openings 
of games played by celebrated masters, showing the best modes of 
taking advantage of the different odds. 


PAWN AND THREE MOVES. 


This odds is rarely given, as it is by no means as interesting or 
instructive as the odds of a Knight, to which it is considered equal. 
The attack, if conducted properly, is almost if not quite over- 
whelming, and the second player in any case has for a long time 
an irksome, disagreeable game, without much chance of releasing 
himself from the thraldom under which he labours. It¢ is under 
stood, however, when the odds of extra moves are given, that the 
first player must not play any of his men beyond his own half of 
the board. Were it otherwise, the receiver of the Pawn and Three 
Moves could force mate at once as follows :— ;

PAwN AND Two MovEs. 


This is a most deceptive degree of odds, for the first player, 
thinking he has a magnificent attack, at first—until warned by ex- 
lence—presses onward with impetuosity, and too late finds he 
Pas overshot the mark, and is himself thrown upon the defensive, 
‘ and with his forces in such a position that they are frequently 
rather a hindrance to each other than otherwise. And in the next 
game, perhaps, anxious to avoid the former mistake, he does not 
play with the necessary confidence, and allows his opponent to get 
an open game, with only a Pawn against his greater skill, the ad- 
vantage of the two moves having vanished. It is hard at first to hit 
the proper mean between impetuosity and timidity. The great 
object of the receiver of the odds should be to keep his game well 
in hand, cramping his opponent by every move, and then to seize 
the inevitable opportunity—inevitable from the nature of the 
opening—of coming down upon him with effect. The following 
openings will be sufficient to instruct the learner in this respect.

PAWN AND MOVE. 


This has always been a favourite description of odds between 
players of nearly equal skill. It may be thought there is little 
ifference between it and the odds of Pawn and Two Moves, but 
this is a mistake; in the latter instance the first player, through 
his extra move, is enabled to exercise a terrible constraint over his 
s forces, rendering it extremely difficult for him to de- 
velop his game; but when giving the Pawn and Move the defend- 
ing player can generally obtain a free game, the superiority of his 
ent consisting not so much in having a great advantage in 
position, but chiefly in his being a Pawn ahead.

340 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


CHAPTER XI. 
ENDINGS OF GAMES. 


Ir is a difficult art to play the endings of es with precision, 
and one that is rarely displayed except by finished practitioners. 
The nature of the calculations required is so different from the 
nature of those necessary in the opening and middle of a game, 
that skill in the latter respect by no means implies the possession 
of it as regards the former, although, of course, it 18 a t step 
towards acquiring it. Tyros are apt to believe that when there 
are few pieces upon the board, or, it may be, three or four Pawns 
only, the calculations are simple and easily made; they forget that 
then the player frequently has not his intuitive judgment of 
position to aid him in at once rejecting an unsuitable move—that 
scarcely any move then can be disposed of at sight, but that each 
one must be thoroughly analyzed, and the result carefully borne 
in mind. Indeed, it is hardly too much to say that more 
“ analysis” is requisite in playing a difficult and evenly balanced 
ending than in most other positions. The “certainty” of an end 
game, the absence of all opportunity for the display of judgment 
—using this word as implying the faculty of playing for a possible, 
though at the time unforeseen, benefit—renders the result 
absolutely dependent upon downright hard work and application; 
and hence the plodding, careful amateur is quite as likely, if not 
more 80, to excel in this department as he whose brilliant dash so 
frequently carries all before it in earlier positions. Time after 
time has an attack been conducted to a successful issue, so far as 
obtaining an advantage sufficient to win is concerned, and, at the 
last moment, the fruits of perhaps hours of intense study have 
been lost through inability to effectually secure them, or, more 
annoying still, through a momentary cessation of the thorough 
attention required to the very last move. From this it will be 
evident that careful study alone is of service in such cases; and 
the amateur who has a previons knowledge of analogous positions 
will have a great advantage over one who is ignorant of them. 
The student, therefore, should not be tempted by the beauty of 
dashing games to devote his attention solely to them ; he should 
study the endings, and endeavour to become proficient in them, 
for then only will he feel that calm security which the possession 
of knowledge insures. ; ; 
In playing over the ilustrative games CORLAANRA in. Lhe sist 


TWO ROOKS. 341 


portion of this work, some idea will have been gained of various 
endings, but not sufficient for obtaining the security just alluded 
to. The information, however, conveyed in this chapter, rightly 
applied, will supply the deficiency. Commencing with the simpler 
checkmates, the learner will be gradually led up to the more ab- 
struse positions, in which one seemingly unimportant move makes 
the difference between success and defeat, or between a victory 
and that most unsatisfactory of terminations—a “draw.” And let 
it be remembered that excellence in the playing of endings can 
best and most easily be attained by study from the “books;” in 
them the place of a teacher can readily be supplied, and the 
student be spared the toil of discovering for himself the correct 
modes of play—or, rather, be spared the vexation of floundering 
on in perplexity as to the soundness of his conclusions, and most 
likely the mortification of ascertaining, when too late, that all he 
knows, opposed to sound skill, amounts to nothing. 


QUEEN AND ROOK. 


To effect this checkmate you must force the King, by a series of 
alternate checks, to the side of the board, and then mate. The 
easiest way of doing this is to keep the Rook one square nearer. to 
the adverse King t your Queen is, because if the latter is the 
nearer piece she is not supported by the Rook, and cannot give 
the alternate checks unless Black’s King is distant; you therefore 
would lose time. (See Diagram 1 at end of chapter. )
