KNIGHT AND PAWN. 


This ending is somewhat similar to the last : White wins ordi- 
narily, but when the Pawn is on the Rook’s files he may draw if 
within reach of it (see Diagram 7). Here it is obvious that 
if White remove the Knight the Pawn falls, and if he attempt 
to protect the latter with the King he gives stalemate, the game 
being drawn in either case.

Rook AND KNIGHT AGAINST Rook. 


These endings are generally drawn. The analyses of the posi- 
tions are too voluminous for insertion. 


THREE MINOR PIECES AGAINST ROOK. 


If two of the pieces are Bishops they will win, as the Rook can 
be won for one of them, and mate follows; butif there are only one 
Bishop and two Knights, the Rook can frequently be exchanged 
for the Bishop, and the game drawn, because two Knights cannot 
mate. 


RooK AND PAWN AGAINST ROOK. 


If the adverse King can get in front of the Pawn the game will 
be drawn; if not, the superior force will win. (See Diagram 12). 
In this position, therefore, by Philidor, the game is drawn in this 
manner :— 


WHITE. BLack 
1. Pto K 5th 1. Rto Q Kt 3rd 
2. Rto Q R 7th 2. R to Q B 3rd 
3. P to K 6th 3. Rto Q B 8th 
4. K to B 6th 4. R to K B 8th (ch) 


And White cannot avoid perpetual check or the loss of the Pawn 
through the King being moved away from its support. The rule 
is for the weaker party to keep his Rook on his third rank, to pre- 
vent the opposite King advancing, until the Pawn reaches its srxth 
square, when the Rook must be played to the seventh or eighth 


350 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


rank. Ifthe adverse Rook can be interposed to the check, and is 
so, the weaker side should exchange Rooks, and he then retains 
the “ opposition” with his King, and draws, as hereafter explained. 


BISHOP AGAINST ROOK, AND 
KNIGHT AGAINST Rook. 


Generally drawn games. The most secure position in the 
former case is with the King on a corner square not commanded 
by the Bishop, and the Bishop on a square next to it. Here you 
move King or Bishop to Knight’s square or Rook’s second, and 
cannot be forced away or mated. ith the King and Knight, 
however, they should be kept away from the corners, the centre 
squares of the side lines being the most safe. 


PAWN AGAINST QUEEN. 


The Queen can always win against a Pawn, except when the 
latter is at the seventh square on either of the Bishops’ or Rooks’ 
files. The following (see Diagram 13) shows the method to be 
pursued :— 


WHITE. BLAck. 
1. Q to K 4th (ch) 1. K to B 7th 
2. Q to Q 3rd 2. K to K 8th 
3. Q to K 3rd (ch) 3. K to Q 8th 
4. K to B 5th 4. K to B 7th 
5. Q to K 2nd 5. K to B 8th 
6. Q to Q B 4th (ch) 6. K to Kt 7th 
7. Q to Q 3rd 7. K to B 8th 
8. Q to Q B ord (ch) 8. K to Q 8th 
9. K to K 4t 9. K to K 7th 
10. Q to K 3rd (ch) 10. K to Q 8th 


11. K to Q 3rd, and wins. 


The object of White, of course, throughout, was to drive the Black 
King in front of the Pawn, to enable him to bring up his own 
King. Ifthe Pawn were not so far advanced, winning would be 
easier, and if the Queen can be played to the eighth square of the

as the two Kings respectively prevent the Bishop being played 
to command White's kK 7th.

370 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


CHAPTER XII. 
BLINDFOLD CHESS. 


It is generally supposed that the faculty of playmg Chess blind- 
fold—or, rather, without seeing the bowrd—ts a git possessed by 
few and cannot be acquired, that it is dependent upon a marvellous 
effort of memory, achieved under peculiar and rare circumstances. 
I believe this is a mistaken idea; I think the art is easily attain- 
able, and that the power of acquiring it is widely extended. It 
depends, in my opinion, not on rn Aetes the general memory, but 
upon the ability to bring before the mind a vivid photograph of the 
position of the pieces on the board at any given time, and this 
ability we see exemplified in many ways. A schoolboy in learning 
a lesson is greatly aided by the image of the page he is studying 
which is fixed upon the “ mind’s eye;” the features of an absent 
friend are recalled in like manner; and the artist finds this power 
of invaluable assistance to him in filling in the details of a hurried 
sketch, the variations of the subject having been too evanescent 
for transfer to paper at the time. I am aware this is but a form of 
memory, but what I wish to call attention to is the fact that it is 
purely the “ memory of sight.” Now, it is admitted that this isa 
more difficult form of remembrance than the memory of hearing 
or of speech, but simply, I conceive, because it is not so much exer- 
cised as the latter. Theeye sees more constantly than the tongue 
speaks or the ear hears, bat it does not usually convey such iis 
tinct and lasting impressions to the brain—it is more frequently 
used without observation. Thus, I think, blindfold Chess is ac- 
counted a great difficulty by reason of the want of continued 
practice on the part of those who have attempted it, and not be- 
cause of its inherent perplexities. 

To illustrate my meaning more thoroughly, and at the same time 
explain what is necessary in playing without sight of the board— 
looking at the men alone cannot in any way assist you—I will con- 
sider what powers a game of Chess calls into operation during its 
progress. It is evident that before making a move a player has to 
analyze the consequences of several moves he may make, the 
possibility of his adversary replying to each in many different 
ways, and the probable result of yack. of the modes of play which 
may be adopted. But in playing over the board the men are not 
allowed to be moved until a decision has been arrived at, and a 
move cannot be recalled when oncemade. Winlowa consequently, 
that at every move the player ia compelled \o cision eek 


BLINDFOLD CHESS. 371 


variations, each involving a distinct series of moves, in his head. 
And it is the faculty of carrying on this examination to a greater 
extent and with more correctness which gives the vic to one 
player over his opponent. We thus see that every one, when con- 
testing me over the board, must necessarily in some measure 
play blindfold, for the sight of the board and men as the game 
stands at the time of consideration is of little assistance—in fact, 
it may almost be said to be an obstruction if the analysis is con- 
tinned very far or embraces many series of combinations, because 
in the mental analysis many of the pieces change their positions, 
and the fact of their remaining on the squares they occupy on the 
board tends to confusion and disturbance of the train of thought 
which is being pursued. Practice, however, it is found, and study, 
extend greatly this power of. examination and analysis. This 
proves that the art of playing blindfold is not dependent upon a 
certain peculiar constitution of the mind—is not a gift, but may be 
ined. But here we arrive at a second point for consideration. 
The player over the board has this great advantage—the position 
of the pieces at the commencement of every analysis is before him, 
and can, in case of doubt or confusion, be referred to at any time; 
whereas the blindfold player is compelled accurately to remember 
the various situations of the men at each move, for if he make a 
single mistake his correctness is at an end, and, in all probability, 
he will either lose the game or break down altogether. Still, this 
power of accurate remembrance is simply an extension of the 
power of analysis with the board before you; for in the latter case, 
when you have examined say five moves ahead, the pieces are 
(mentally) completely changed in their positions, and you must 
remember the new situations they occupy. From the foregoing I 
think it is evident that blindfold play is only continuing the same 
powers that are required for play over the board. 

It may, however, be argued that some of the best players could 
never play blindfold. I reply that they did do so really, although 
not apparently, and because they did not do so ostensibly is no 
argument that they could not—they did not try to do it, else they 
would have succeeded ; for by practice they would have overcome 
the difficulty of separating the variations they had examined and 
rejected from the moves really adopted. This is the great difficulty, 
and is where the beginner mostly fails; but the effort to achieve 
his object will not have harmed him, it will rather have strength- 
ened his mind by the exercise; and I believe, therefore, that the 
advice usually given—never to attempt to play blindfold unless 
you are not only a good player, but also much accustomed to play- 
ing over the board—is founded upon a fallacy. It is true, un- 
doubtedly, that he to whom the positions of the pieces in various 
situations, their bearing upon certain squares, and the relation of 
the latter to each other are already familiar will have leas troukd\s 
in learning the art; but this is only the advantage of greeter ox 


e 


perience, which would be quite as apparent, if not more Ww, 


372 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


ordinary play. A boy is not made less capable of studying and 
understanding algebra and trigonometry because he has been 
exercised in mental arithmetic, or in the solution of previously un- 
known. mathematical problems at sight; on the contrary, the 
discipline will have greatly strengthened his powers of calculation 
and reason, and rendered him more able quickly and correctly to 
solve the questions placed before him. Similarly, I believe, the 
habit of studying games and positions without the board is advan- 
tageous, and will tend to a more ready perception of what lines of 
play are suitable or the reverse, and to their adoption or rejection 
at pleasure. In these remarks I wish it to be understood that I 
do not refer to the achievements of Morphy, Paulsen, Blackburne, 
&c., in blindfold Chess—playing ten or twelve es simultane- 
ous! inst as many different opponents, and playing them in as 
fine a style as over the board. To do this necessitates great wer 
of concentrating the thoughts, and also extraordinary aptitude for 
the game. It is, however, by no means so difficult as it seems to 
play three or four games at once without seeing the board, although 
the strength of play will be much inferior. 

The first thing necessary in learning to play blindfold—the very 
A BC of the art—is to be thoroughly acquainted with the notation 
of the e. This the student will beif he have attentively played 
through the revious chapters; and he should now take one of the 
shortest of the end games in the next chapter, set up the position 
on the board, and study it carefully until he thinks he can re- 
member the exact position of every piece and its relation to each 
of the others. This being done, it should be played over on the 
board carefully, and then put aside altogether, and, if possible, 
dismissed from the thoughts for some hours. At the expiration 
of that time you should sit down quietly and endeavour to recall 
the position accurately in your mind. (A beginner will find ita 
great assistance to close his eyes while doing this, as enabling him 
more readily and easily to concentrate his ideas.) It may be found 
that the thorough recalling of the situation of the pieces is diff- 
cult of accomplishment at first—this is to be expected; but do not 
despair. Try to do it until you are certain you cannot succeed; 
then—and then only—refer to the book and see where you have 
failed. Again study the position, and again putit aside for a time. 
When you next sit down you will most probably remember it per- 
fectly. Play it out in your mind, as you previously did over the 
board, and take care to carry each variation of the position in 
memory. If you should fail again do not be discouraged. Nothi 
can be accomplished without labour. Persevere, therefore, 
after a few attempts you will succeed. Then take another end 

e, and treat it in a similar manner—it will not be so difficult 
this time; you will remember more easily and more clearly, and 


will improve at every succeeding eitempt, until, at the expiratio® 
tha 


of a few weeks, you will be able to manage even one K ngest 


~"the end games. Now you will be fitted ior & \snggr Sign. 


BLINDFOLD CHESS. 373 


After playing a e with a friend, as soon as you have leisure sit 
down with the board and endeavour to play it over by yourself, 
writing down the moves as you proceed. A great assistance in 
this will be your remembrance of the variations you analyzed 
during the play, but do not confuse them with the moves really 
made—accustom yourself, while thus trying to recollect the game, 
never to move a piece until you are quite certain it is the move 
which was made. Whenever Phe witness a partie between two 
good players remember it and write it down afterwards. Ina 
short time you will find yourself able to dispense with the board 
and men, able to copy the game merely from recollection in your 
mind. By this time you will have made a great advance towards 
ability to play blindfold, but before attempting to do so it will be 
necessary to study attentively the numbering, so to say, of the 
board, the actions of the various pieces, and their bearings upon 
the different squares. 

It may be imagined that the board requires no study. Nor 
would it do so were our notation expressed altogether by numbers, 
but the fact of the names applied to the files being the names of 
the pieces necessitates the acquirement of so much knowledge as 
will enable you at once to understand the distance between any of 
them that may be mentioned. The utility of this will be per- 
ceived subsequently ; at present we will attend only to the result. 
Take the first squares of any two files—we want to ascertain their 
distance from each other. Suppose we have K sq and Q Kt sq; 
on looking at the board we find they are three squares from eac 
other. Similarly K Kt sq is two squares from K sq. It will there- 
fore be perceived that d R sq is distant from K KR sq seven 
squares, from K Kt sq six, from K B sq five, from K sq four, from 

sq three, from Q B sq two, and from Q Kt sq one square. Q 

t sq is distant from K RB sq six squares, from K Kt sq five, from 
K B sq four, from K sq three, from Q sq two, from a) B sq one, 
and from Q EB sq one; Q sq is distant from K RB sq four, from 
K Kt sq three, from K B sq two, from K sq one, from Q B sq one, 
from Q kt sq two, and from Q KR sq three; K B sq is distant from 
K RB sq two, from K Kt sq one, from K sq one, trom Q sq two, 
from Q B sq three, from Q Kt sq four, and from Q KB aq five. 
(I need not particularize the rest, as the respective distances will 
be obvious to the student.) It will also be evident that the K and 
Q squares are one distant from each other, the K B and Q B 
squares three distant, the K Kt and Q Kt five distant, and the K 
B and Q RB seven distant—all odd numbers. All this must be 
thoroughly understood, so that the “number of distance,” as it 
may be termed, between any two files can be immediately, and 
even unconsciously, appreciated. 

Now we come to the action and bearings of the pieces. The 
King’s move, being confined to one square at a time, in any direc- 
tion, presents not the slightest difficulty, and may be at once dis- 
missed. The same is the case with the Rook's move, ma en 


374 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


commands only ranks and files, and therefore no consideration 
whatever is requisite to understand its bearing upon any position. 
But it is otherwise with the Bishop; its diagonal course causes its 
position as regards the ranks and files to vary at every move, and 
te know the squares it commands calls into action the information 
before given respecting the board and the “ number of distance.” 
Place the Bishop on K sq, and we see that it may be played to 
K RB 4th, K Kt 3rd, K B 2nd, Q 2nd, Q B 3rd, Q Kt 4th, and QR 
5th, the number of any square it commands being always one 
more than the distance of the file of that square from K sq. This 
princi le applies to every square on the d, and from it can 
deduced the following rule :—To find the square on any par- 
ticular file commanded, add or subtract the “number of distance” 
of that file to or from the number of the square on which the 
Bishop stands, never, of course, in the addition exceeding eight, or 
in the subtraction going below one. The following examples will 
make this clear :—Bishop on Q R 4th; what square will it com- 
mand on QB file? The “number of distance” is 2; add this to 
the number of the square, 4, and we have 6; subtract it and we 
have 2; the Bishop, therefore, commands Q B 2nd and Q B 6th. 
Bishop on Q 7th; what square will it command on K Kt file? 
Number of square 7; “number of distance” 3; adding we have 
10, subtracting we have 4; the former is above 8, and is therefore 
thrown out—consequently the Bishop commands K Kt 4th. 
Bishop on Q Kt 2nd; what will it command on K B file? Number 
of square 2; “number of distance” 4; adding we get 6, sub- 
tracting we get “minus 2;” the latter is below one, and is 
thrown out—the Bishop commands K B 6th. Bishop on K Kt 
sq; what will it command on K R file? Number of square 1; 
“number of distance”? 1; adding, the result is 2, subtracting, 
it is 0; the latter is thrown out, being below one, and the Bishop 
commands K R 2nd. It will be obvious that, knowing the bear- 
ings of the Rook and the Bishop, we are acquainted with those of 
the Queen, which are merely a compound or union of the two 
former. We have now, therefore, to consider the peculiar move of 
the Knight, and, although it is peculiar, the student should not 
run away with the idea that it is more perplexing than any other. 
Advice has been given by writers who were not practically familiar 
with blindfold Chess, to the effect that it is frequently good for the 
non-seeing player to give up his Rooks for the opponent’s Knights, 
the moves of the latter being so difficult of calculation that, even 
at some sacrifice of power, it is advantageous to get rid of them. 
By doing this, however, the blindfold player at once gives up & 
double “ exchange,” which is equal to at least @ minor piece, and 
he ought consequently, beyond all question, to lose the game. 
The action of the Knight is no more puzzling to one ploying 
without the board than it is to one with the board before him. 
only requires care and sttention—perticular eienion,t t, but 
little, if any, more than is req to folkow Lhe yrdusdis Wada 


BLINDFOLD CHESS. 8375 


or defences of the Bishops and the Queen. There are certain 
fixed rules appertaining to the Knight’s move, and when once 
they are thoroughly comprehended and fixed in the mind, all un- 
certainty is at an end. These rules, however, require a greater 
effort to insure their accurate remembrance, involving, as they do, 
longer, though not more difficult, calculations. Let one of the 
Knights be placed on, say K B 3rd, a position more frequen 
occupied by it than any other. From this square it comman 
eight other squares, and on consideration we ascertain the follow- 
ing rule :—On the adjoining files it commands the second square 
in number from the one on which it stands, either adding or sub- 
tracting, while on the second files away it commands the next 
numbers. For instance, from K B 3rd it commands K 3+2=K 5th, 
K 3—2=K ag, K Kt3+2=K Kt 5th, and K Kt 3—2=K Kt aq; 
it also commands Q 3+1=Q 4th, Q 3 —1=Q 2nd, K BR 3rd+1= 
K B 4th, and K K 8rd—1=K R 2nd. If the addition make 
above eight, the Knight cannot be played forward on the file, and 
if the subtraction leave less than one it cannot be played back- 
ward. It will be easily remembered that on the third and fourth 
files or ranks from the sides of the board the Knight commands 
eight squares, the full extent of its power, as in each direction it 
can be played to the second file away. On the second rank or 
file from the side, however, a movement to one side would take 
the Knight off the board; consequently two squares to which it 
might otherwise be played are cut off, and it commands six only. 
In like manner, on either of the side ranks or files, two files, 
equa to four squares, are taken from its power, leaving but four 
which it can command. This is the general rule, and the follow- 
ing are the only exceptions—the sixteen squares which form the 
four corners of the board. On the squares diagonally next to the 
corners, two files at right angles are cut off from the power of 
moving—the Knight, therefore, on each player’s K Kt 2nd and Q 
Kt 2nd commands four squares; on the eight side squares next 
to the corners, on each player’s K RB 2nd, K Kt sq, Q Kt sq, and 
Q B 2nd, it commands three squares ; on the corner squares, each 
player’s K R sq and Q & aq, the Knight commands only two 
uares. From the foregoing it is evident that the direct action 
of the Knight on the move can be at once ascertained, and, con- 
sequently, his action and the squares it will command after any 
number of moves. But this is not sufficient. It may frequently 
occur that the assistance of a Knight is requisite to enable you to 
obtain the utmost advantage from a position; should that piece, 
however, be at a distance, it necessitates a calculation as to the 
shortest number of moves in which it may be brought up to aid 
effectually in the attack, and a ready means of ascertaining this 
will save a considerable expenditure of thought and examination 
to the player. We know that the Queen, Rook, and Bishop, from 
any position, can be played in one move to command my cher 
square on the board—of course on the supposition Lak Share we 


376 THE BOOK OF CHESS. 


no pieces, either of your own or your opponents, which will interfere 
with the range of either. This arises from the fact of their moves 
ing always in straight lines and unrestricted as to distance. With 
the Knight it is different in both ways—its move is not straight, 
and it is confined in distance—hence it will command various 
squares in various numbers of moves; and without some formula 
for guidance these numbers must always be separately calculated, 
with all the tendency to confusion from the greater le and 
complexity of the analysis required. On examining the it 
will be found that each square has 4 relation of position to every 
other square in one of three ways—this relation 1s either straight, 
diagonal, or a union of the two, and in each the power of 
command by the Knight of squares equally distant is different. 
If the Knight be placed on K it is ascertained by i 
that from there it will command the next square in a 8 ht line 
in two moves, the second square away in one move, the third m 
two, the fourth in one, the in two, the sixth in three, and the 
seventh, or end square, in four moves. It is also ascertained that 
it will command the next square diagonally in three moves (this is 
from the corner only, in any other position the next square diago- 
nally is commanded in one move), the second square away in three 
moves, the third in one, the fourth, fifth, and sixth in three, and 
the seventh, or opposite corner square, in five moves. These 
results, with the one exception pointed out, are invariable in any 
part of the board. There is, therefore, only the third relation of 
squares, the union of a straight and a diagonal move, remaining to 
be considered. And here we must again bring in the “ numbers 
of distance” to our aid. Each square, as situated with regard to any 
other square, has two “numbers of distance,” that of the files and 
that of the ranks; but it should be observed that the real distance 
between them is no more than the greater of these numbers, that 
is, the King can be played from the one to the other in just as many 
moves as the greater “number of distance.” From this it is manl- 
fest that all the squares along any rank or file have exactly the 
same greatest “number of distance” in relation to another square 
from which they all have also an equal or less “number of dis- 
tance.” For instance, every square on Queen’s Rook’s file is seven 
distant from K Rsq. Again, on Queen’s file each square is four 
distant from K BR sq, until we reach Q 6th: this square has a 
eater “number of distance,” five—hence it is five from K R sq. 
earing this in mind, on examining the board we learn that, what- 
ever position any squares may hold towards another, those having 
the same greatest “number of distance” will be commanded by 
the Knight, starting from the latter in each case, in the same 
number of moves, if they are of the same colour as each other. 
We will first take those which are white, the colour of K Bag, 
from which we begin. 
It must be remembered that now the Gsggosl rows are not 
taken into consideration. A\\ aquerea on the weme agonsihare ka

two “numbers of distance” equal. The white squares three dis- 
tant are commanded in one move, those four distant in one, those 
five distant in three, those six distant in three, and those seven 
distant in three also. Now, coming to the black squares, different 
from the one from which we start, we find those which are two 
distant are commanded without moving, from K B sq itself; those 
three distant are commanded in two, those four distant in two, 
those five distant also in two, those six distant in two [or four], 
and those seven distant in four. The black squares six distant, it 
will be seen, vary as regards the “number of command.” If the 
two “numbers of distance” added together make eleven, the 
Knight will only command that square in four moves, but if they 
make nine or seven it will command it in two. In the application 
of the above figures it is necessary to know whether the square it 
is desired to command is white or black; this is easily ascertained 
by adding the two “ numbers of distance; ” if the result is an even 
number, the colour will be the same as that of the square from 
which you start, if odd the colour will be different. For the con- 
venience of the student I think it better to tabulate the foregoing - 
formulz, so that they may be more readily committed to memory.

The application of this table, as regards the straight and diagonal 
lines of squares, is apparent, and I need only illustrate it respectin. 
the union of the two. Suppose your Knight is on Q KB 3rd, an 
you wish to know the shortest number of moves in which it will 
command K R 7th; the “numbers of distance,” 7 and 4, make 11, 
an odd number, therefore the colours of the two squares are dif- 
ferent. The greatest “number of distance” is 7, and on referring 
to the table, under “ different colour,” we find it will take four 
moves—say, 1. Kt to Q Kt sq; 2. Kt to Q 2nd; 3. Kt toK B 3rd; 
and 4. Kt to K Kt 5th. Again, ifthe Knight is on K B 3rd, and you 
wish it to command Q B &th, in how many moves will it do aa? 
“Numbers of distance ” 5 and 3 = 8, same colour , greskert “ nom-

ber of distance” 5. Referring to the table, under “same colour,” 
we have 3—say, 1. Kt to Q 2nd; 2. Kt toQ B 4th; and 3. Ktto 
Q Kt 6th. There are now only the action and bearing of the Pawn 
left for consideration, and these are too simple to require eomment. 
The whole of the details given above must be thoroughly studied, 
must be committed to memory accurately, must be practised con- 
tinually, until the student knows at once—intuitively, as it were; 
similarly to the mental application of the simple rules of arith- 
metic—everything he wishes to know respecting any piece or 
number of pieces. It must not be supposed, however, that when 
this facility of calculating the bearings of a piece has been attained 
it will of itself enable one to play blindfold. All these calculations 
are merely subordinate; they are extremely useful in their place, 
disciplining the mind, and accustoming it to more readily grasp 
the object or probable results of a move; but they can be done 
without. The great secret is the power of bringing a p 
of the board and the various mutations of the pieces before 
inner sight, and, although the foregoing rules are of great aasist- 
ance in doing this, yet nothing but practice will really sveil. 
However, as I said before, I believe every one is more or less 
gifted with the power spoken of, and that it simply requires culti- 
vation. ‘“ Many a time and oft,” as the old phrase says, have I, in 
company with my friend Henry Jones, sauntered along the green 
winding lanes or through the sweet-smelling hay-fields, playing 
over some fine contest between such men as Staun 
Cochrane, Morphy and Liéwenthal, or Anderssen and Harrwits. 
Many a time have we thus beguiled the tedium of a long railway 
journey; many a time, most enjoyable of all, have we wan 
over the breezy downs till we came across the white tents of the 
cricket-field, and have thrown ourselves upon the yielding yet 
springy turf, and, while we watched the changing fortune of the 
game before us, and criticised the varied skill, have at the same 
time carried on a fierce and stubborn encounter in our minds, 
yielding ourselves entirely to the delightful abandon of the moment, 
and gaining rest and recreation for ody and mind alike. Such 
memories as these cast a halo of pleasure over my thoughts of the 
royal game, and most sincerely do I hope many of my readers will 
in their turn appreciate and enjoy them. 


CHESS STUDIES. 379 


CHAPTER XIII. 


CHESS STUDIES. 


Tue following Chess Studies are inserted for the amusement 
of the reader when he is wearied, as he is apt to become, by a 
study of the endless variations arising in an examination of the 
analysis of an opening or @ game. They are selected principally 
from the parties of Mr. Morphy, than whom a more dashing and 
at the same time sound player perhaps never lived, and I believe 
will be found at once brilliant and instructive in the highest 
degree. It will be seen that, with one exception, the celebrated 
“Indian Problem,” I have not inserted any problems, properly so 
called, in which mate is to be given by force in a certain number of 
moves, and for thefollowing among other reasons :— Problems, being 
manufactured, and seldom springing from or leading to positions 
likely to occur in an actual game, do not, I imagine, excite that 
amount of interest which is felt in a fine study, which either may 
be useful subsequently or has actually been played over the 
board; the latter seems to have a kind of personal interest con- 
nected with it which is wanting to the former, and therefore gives 
more satisfaction in the contemplation. And so many fine pro- 
blems are published now-a-days, copies of which can be readil 
obtained, that I had less hesitation in discarding them than 
might otherwise have had. With these few remarks I intro- 
duce my readers to the following pages, which will be found to 
contain what may be termed a mine of wealth, feeling sure that if 
attentively studied they will afford the highest gratification.
